Well, yes, absolutely. I used to love those mentoring calls. I’d set aside every Thursday morning for them. But now... I just haven't been showing up. I haven't answered any of their emails either. Well, I wouldn't say I'm down all the time. It's more like... I'm just overwhelmed, you know? But not really depressed like they talk about on those commercials. Speaking of commercials, have you noticed how many of them are for medications these days? It's incredible. Well, it's... I suppose it's a bit of both, really. I just don't feel like doing much of anything. It's like the color has been drained out of everything, you know? Not just the mentoring. We were just talking the week before about his daughter starting at the state university, which is wonderful because they were so worried about the tuition costs, and I told him that the in-state options are just as rigorous if you look at the right departments... \n\nBut, um... wait. What was I... right, the timeline. Yes, about two months. Ever since that day she fell, everything at home has just completely changed, and I've been stuck like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve lost interest in things you used to enjoy, like mentoring or other activities?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, absolutely. I used to love those mentoring calls. I’d set aside every Thursday morning for them. But now... I just haven't been showing up. I haven't answered any of their emails either. And it's not just that. It's the house, too. I used to handle all the bills, the upkeep, the routines—just did it without even thinking. Now, the bills are just sitting in a pile on the entryway table. It’s a beautiful table, actually—solid mahogany, we found it at an estate sale in Oak Park back in... oh, it must have been '98 or '99. The craftsmanship on those older pieces is just remarkable. You don't see dovetail joints like that anymore, everything now is just held together with cheap glue and staples. I remember the man who sold it to us, he had the most fascinating collection of vintage pocket watches...\n\nBut, I'm sorry, I'm wandering again. Yes, the house. The routines are just slipping badly. I can't seem to manage the basic upkeep or the bills, and I've completely dropped all my community commitments and the mentoring. I just can't seem to care about any of it right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or in a low mood most of the time since your wife’s fall?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say I'm down all the time. Well, my appetite... I haven't really noticed a big difference, to be honest. I mean, I'm still eating. But speaking of food, the hospital food they've been giving my wife is just atrocious. I remember the man who sold it to us, he had the most fascinating collection of vintage pocket watches...\n\nBut, I'm sorry, I'm wandering again. Yes, the house. The routines are just slipping badly. I can't seem to manage the basic upkeep or the bills, and I've completely dropped all my community commitments and the mentoring. I just can't seem to care about any of it right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or in a low mood most of the time since your wife’s fall?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say I'm down all the time. It's more like... I'm just overwhelmed, you know? But not really depressed like they talk about on those commercials. Speaking of commercials, have you noticed how many of them are for medications these days? It's incredible. Every time I turn on the evening news—which I've been watching more of lately, usually the local broadcast with Jim Henderson. He's been the anchor for twenty years. I actually met him once at a charity dinner for the rotary club. He’s much taller in person than he looks on television, and he was telling this hilarious story about his teleprompter breaking right in the middle of a live broadcast... \n\nBut, um... what was the question? Oh, right, feeling down. No, I don't think it's that. It's more just the crying out of nowhere and snapping at things, like I mentioned. I just feel so out of control of everything.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you no longer get any pleasure or satisfaction out of things you used to enjoy, like mentoring or other activities?\n\nPatient: Well, like I said before... I just haven't been doing them at all. The mentoring, I mean. So I suppose I'm not getting pleasure out of it, because I'm not even there. I just... I don't feel the pull to do it. Which is a shame, really, because the young man I was mentoring, David, he was just starting to get the hang of the new software systems they use in the engineering department. When I was working, we did everything by hand, on drafting tables. You had to really know your geometry back then. I still have my old drafting set, actually, from when I was at university. The compass is solid brass, German-made. They just don't manufacture instruments with that kind of precision anymore. I was thinking of giving it to David, actually, as a graduation present, because he... \n\nWait, sorry. No, I'm just not doing the activities anymore. I just don't have the energy or the interest to even try right now. Everything just feels like too much effort.\n\nDoctor: When you say you don’t feel the pull to do the mentoring anymore, is it because you no longer enjoy it, or more that you just don’t feel like doing anything at all?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... I suppose it's a bit of both, really. I just don't feel like doing much of anything. It's like the color has been drained out of everything, you know? Not just the mentoring. Even my morning coffee doesn't taste right anymore. I used to buy these specific beans from a little roaster down on 4th Street. The owner, Marcus, he travels to Colombia every year to source them directly. He was telling me once about the altitude and the soil acidity and how it completely changes the flavor profile. Fascinating stuff. I actually tried growing my own coffee plant in the sunroom a few years back, but the humidity in this part of the country just isn't right for it. The leaves all turned yellow and dropped off by November... \n\nBut anyway, yes. I just... I don't feel like doing anything at all. It's not that I don't like the mentoring anymore, it's just that the thought of getting dressed and going out and talking to people... it's just too much. I'd rather just sit.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight since your wife’s fall?\n\nPatient: Well, my appetite... I haven't really noticed a big difference, to be honest. I mean, I'm still eating. But speaking of food, the hospital food they've been giving my wife is just atrocious. I don't know how anyone is expected to heal eating that sort of thing. Yesterday they brought her this... I think it was supposed to be meatloaf, but it looked more like a wet sponge. I ended up going down to the cafeteria to get her a sandwich, but even that was wrapped in so much plastic it took me five minutes just to get it open. It reminds me of those TV dinners we used to have back in the seventies, you know? Sleep? No, I wouldn't say there's been any changes. I sleep about the same as I always have. I've never been one of those people who needs eight hours, anyway. My father was the same way. He used to wake up at four in the morning every single day, without an alarm clock, just to go down to the docks. He was a longshoreman for forty-five years. Worked right through the strike in '71. He always said the secret to a good day's work was a bowl of oatmeal and being up before the sun. I remember he had this old thermos, plaid, with a cork stopper, and he'd fill it with black tea... 

But, uh, no. My sleep is fine. I haven't noticed any trouble with it.

I used to love those mentoring calls. I’d set aside every Thursday morning for them. But now... I just haven't been showing up. I haven't answered any of their emails either. And it's not just that. It's the house, too. I used to handle all the bills, the upkeep, the routines—just did it without even thinking. Now, the bills are just sitting in a pile on the entryway table. It’s a beautiful table, actually—solid mahogany, we found it at an estate sale in Oak Park back in... oh, it must have been '98 or '99. The craftsmanship on those older pieces is just remarkable. You don't see dovetail joints like that anymore, everything now is just held together with cheap glue and staples. I remember the man who sold it to us, he had the most fascinating collection of vintage pocket watches...\n\nBut, I'm sorry, I'm wandering again. Yes, the house. The routines are just slipping badly. I can't seem to manage the basic upkeep or the bills, and I've completely dropped all my community commitments and the mentoring. I just can't seem to care about any of it right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or in a low mood most of the time since your wife’s fall?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say I'm down all the time. It's more like... I'm just overwhelmed, you know? But not really depressed like they talk about on those commercials. Speaking of commercials, have you noticed how many of them are for medications these days? It's incredible. Every time I turn on the evening news—which I've been watching more of lately, usually the local broadcast with Jim Henderson. He's been the anchor for twenty years. I actually met him once at a charity dinner for the rotary club. He’s much taller in person than he looks on television, and he was telling this hilarious story about his teleprompter breaking right in the middle of a live broadcast... \n\nBut, um... what was the question? Oh, right, feeling down. No, I don't think it's that. It's more just the crying out of nowhere and snapping at things, like I mentioned. I just feel so out of control of everything.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you no longer get any pleasure or satisfaction out of things you used to enjoy, like mentoring or other activities?\n\nPatient: Well, like I said before... I just haven't been doing them at all. The mentoring, I mean. So I suppose I'm not getting pleasure out of it, because I'm not even there. I just... I don't feel the pull to do it. Which is a shame, really, because the young man I was mentoring, David, he was just starting to get the hang of the new software systems they use in the engineering department. When I was working, we did everything by hand, on drafting tables. You had to really know your geometry back then. I still have my old drafting set, actually, from when I was at university. The compass is solid brass, German-made. They just don't manufacture instruments with that kind of precision anymore. I was thinking of giving it to David, actually, as a graduation present, because he... \n\nWait, sorry. No, I'm just not doing the activities anymore. I just don't have the energy or the interest to even try right now. Everything just feels like too much effort.\n\nDoctor: When you say you don’t feel the pull to do the mentoring anymore, is it because you no longer enjoy it, or more that you just don’t feel like doing anything at all?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... I suppose it's a bit of both, really. I just don't feel like doing much of anything. It's like the color has been drained out of everything, you know? Not just the mentoring. Even my morning coffee doesn't taste right anymore. I used to buy these specific beans from a little roaster down on 4th Street. The owner, Marcus, he travels to Colombia every year to source them directly. He was telling me once about the altitude and the soil acidity and how it completely changes the flavor profile. Fascinating stuff. I actually tried growing my own coffee plant in the sunroom a few years back, but the humidity in this part of the country just isn't right for it. The leaves all turned yellow and dropped off by November... \n\nBut anyway, yes. I just... I don't feel like doing anything at all. It's not that I don't like the mentoring anymore, it's just that the thought of getting dressed and going out and talking to people... it's just too much. I'd rather just sit.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight since your wife’s fall?\n\nPatient: Well, my appetite... I haven't really noticed a big difference, to be honest. I mean, I'm still eating. But speaking of food, the hospital food they've been giving my wife is just atrocious. I ended up going down to the cafeteria to get her a sandwich, but even that was wrapped in so much plastic it took me five minutes just to get it open. Guilty? No, I don't... I wouldn't say guilty. I mean, I'm doing what I can, going to the hospital and all. But hospitals, they really are something else these days. I was looking at the equipment in her room yesterday, and it's all touch screens and wireless monitors. It got me thinking about when my brother had his appendix out in, oh, it must have been 1982. The machines back then were massive, covered in dials and switches, making all sorts of racket. The nurses had to write everything down on these thick paper charts. I remember this one nurse, she had the most impeccable handwriting, like calligraphy. She told me she used to practice by copying out poems by Robert Frost. 'Stopping by Woods on a Snowy Evening,' that was her favorite... 

Anyway, what was it? Guilt? No, I don't think I feel guilty. I just feel... tired, mostly. But not guilty, no.

Ever since that day she fell, everything at home has just completely changed, and I've been stuck like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve lost interest in things you used to enjoy, like mentoring or other activities?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, absolutely. I used to love those mentoring calls. I’d set aside every Thursday morning for them. But now... I just haven't been showing up. I haven't answered any of their emails either. And it's not just that. It's the house, too. I used to handle all the bills, the upkeep, the routines—just did it without even thinking. Now, the bills are just sitting in a pile on the entryway table. It’s a beautiful table, actually—solid mahogany, we found it at an estate sale in Oak Park back in... oh, it must have been '98 or '99. The craftsmanship on those older pieces is just remarkable. You don't see dovetail joints like that anymore, everything now is just held together with cheap glue and staples. I remember the man who sold it to us, he had the most fascinating collection of vintage pocket watches...\n\nBut, I'm sorry, I'm wandering again. Yes, the house. The routines are just slipping badly. I can't seem to manage the basic upkeep or the bills, and I've completely dropped all my community commitments and the mentoring. I just can't seem to care about any of it right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or in a low mood most of the time since your wife’s fall?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say I'm down all the time. It's more like... I'm just overwhelmed, you know? But not really depressed like they talk about on those commercials. Speaking of commercials, have you noticed how many of them are for medications these days? It's incredible. Every time I turn on the evening news—which I've been watching more of lately, usually the local broadcast with Jim Henderson. He's been the anchor for twenty years. I actually met him once at a charity dinner for the rotary club. He’s much taller in person than he looks on television, and he was telling this hilarious story about his teleprompter breaking right in the middle of a live broadcast... \n\nBut, um... what was the question? Oh, right, feeling down. No, I don't think it's that. It's more just the crying out of nowhere and snapping at things, like I mentioned. I just feel so out of control of everything.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you no longer get any pleasure or satisfaction out of things you used to enjoy, like mentoring or other activities?\n\nPatient: Well, like I said before... I just haven't been doing them at all. The mentoring, I mean. So I suppose I'm not getting pleasure out of it, because I'm not even there. I just... I don't feel the pull to do it. Which is a shame, really, because the young man I was mentoring, David, he was just starting to get the hang of the new software systems they use in the engineering department. When I was working, we did everything by hand, on drafting tables. You had to really know your geometry back then. I still have my old drafting set, actually, from when I was at university. The compass is solid brass, German-made. They just don't manufacture instruments with that kind of precision anymore. I was thinking of giving it to David, actually, as a graduation present, because he... \n\nWait, sorry. No, I'm just not doing the activities anymore. I just don't have the energy or the interest to even try right now. Everything just feels like too much effort.\n\nDoctor: When you say you don’t feel the pull to do the mentoring anymore, is it because you no longer enjoy it, or more that you just don’t feel like doing anything at all?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... I suppose it's a bit of both, really. I just don't feel like doing much of anything. It's like the color has been drained out of everything, you know? Not just the mentoring. Even my morning coffee doesn't taste right anymore. I used to buy these specific beans from a little roaster down on 4th Street. The owner, Marcus, he travels to Colombia every year to source them directly. He was telling me once about the altitude and the soil acidity and how it completely changes the flavor profile. Fascinating stuff. I actually tried growing my own coffee plant in the sunroom a few years back, but the humidity in this part of the country just isn't right for it. The leaves all turned yellow and dropped off by November... \n\nBut anyway, yes. I just... I don't feel like doing anything at all. It's not that I don't like the mentoring anymore, it's just that the thought of getting dressed and going out and talking to people... it's just too much. I'd rather just sit.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight since your wife’s fall?\n\nPatient: Well, my appetite... I haven't really noticed a big difference, to be honest. I mean, I'm still eating. But speaking of food, the hospital food they've been giving my wife is just atrocious. I don't know how anyone is expected to heal eating that sort of thing. Yesterday they brought her this... I think it was supposed to be meatloaf, but it looked more like a wet sponge. I ended up going down to the cafeteria to get her a sandwich, but even that was wrapped in so much plastic it took me five minutes just to get it open. It reminds me of those TV dinners we used to have back in the seventies, you know? The ones in the aluminum trays where the peas would always spill over into the cobbler... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, my weight. No, I don't think my weight has changed. I haven't stepped on a scale, but my trousers still fit the same.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep lately, like trouble falling asleep, waking up during the night, or sleeping more than usual?\n\nPatient: Sleep? No, I wouldn't say there's been any changes. I sleep about the same as I always have. I've never been one of those people who needs eight hours, anyway. My father was the same way. He used to wake up at four in the morning every single day, without an alarm clock, just to go down to the docks. He was a longshoreman for forty-five years. Worked right through the strike in '71. He always said the secret to a good day's work was a bowl of oatmeal and being up before the sun. I remember he had this old thermos, plaid, with a cork stopper, and he'd fill it with black tea... \n\nBut, uh, no. My sleep is fine. I haven't noticed any trouble with it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or felt like you’re not doing enough, especially in relation to your wife or other responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Guilty? No, I don't... I wouldn't say guilty. I mean, I'm doing what I can, going to the hospital and all. But hospitals, they really are something else these days. I was looking at the equipment in her room yesterday, and it's all touch screens and wireless monitors. It got me thinking about when my brother had his appendix out in, oh, it must have been 1982. The machines back then were massive, covered in dials and switches, making all sorts of racket. The nurses had to write everything down on these thick paper charts. I remember this one nurse, she had the most impeccable handwriting, like calligraphy. She told me she used to practice by copying out poems by Robert Frost. 'Stopping by Woods on a Snowy Evening,' that was her favorite... \n\nAnyway, what was it? Guilt? No, I don't think I feel guilty. I just feel... tired, mostly. But not guilty, no.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. But not guilty, no.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed that you’ve been moving more slowly than usual, or have others commented that you seem slowed down?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 68 / not_in_workforce / graduate\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Moving slower? No, I haven't really noticed anything like that. I get around alright. But you know who is moving slow these days? The construction crews on Interstate 95. I had to take a detour coming here today, and it was just a parking lot. They've had those orange barrels out since, gosh, April? It reminds me of this project I consulted on before I retired—I have my master's in urban planning, you know. We were doing a transit expansion over in the next county, and the zoning delays were just a nightmare. We had a proposal stuck in committee for three months because of a dispute over a historic retaining wall. The head of the historical society, this woman named Beatrice, used to come to every single town hall meeting with these giant binders full of sepia photographs. She had this one picture from 1912 of a horse-drawn carriage that had lost a wheel right in front of the wall... 

Wait, sorry, I lost my train of thought. What did you ask me? Oh, right, me moving slow. No, no one's said I look slowed down or anything. I'm walking at the same pace I always do.

I used to love those mentoring calls. I’d set aside every Thursday morning for them. But now... I just haven't been showing up. I haven't answered any of their emails either. And it's not just that. It's the house, too. I used to handle all the bills, the upkeep, the routines—just did it without even thinking. Now, the bills are just sitting in a pile on the entryway table. It’s a beautiful table, actually—solid mahogany, we found it at an estate sale in Oak Park back in... oh, it must have been '98 or '99. The craftsmanship on those older pieces is just remarkable. You don't see dovetail joints like that anymore, everything now is just held together with cheap glue and staples. I can't seem to manage the basic upkeep or the bills, and I've completely dropped all my community commitments and the mentoring. I just can't seem to care about any of it right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or in a low mood most of the time since your wife’s fall?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say I'm down all the time. I just feel so out of control of everything.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you no longer get any pleasure or satisfaction out of things you used to enjoy, like mentoring or other activities?\n\nPatient: Well, like I said before... I just haven't been doing them at all. The mentoring, I mean. So I suppose I'm not getting pleasure out of it, because I'm not even there. I just... I don't feel the pull to do it. Which is a shame, really, because the young man I was mentoring, David, he was just starting to get the hang of the new software systems they use in the engineering department. When I was working, we did everything by hand, on drafting tables. You had to really know your geometry back then. I still have my old drafting set, actually, from when I was at university. The compass is solid brass, German-made. They just don't manufacture instruments with that kind of precision anymore. I was thinking of giving it to David, actually, as a graduation present, because he... \n\nWait, sorry. No, I'm just not doing the activities anymore. I just don't have the energy or the interest to even try right now. Everything just feels like too much effort.\n\nDoctor: When you say you don’t feel the pull to do the mentoring anymore, is it because you no longer enjoy it, or more that you just don’t feel like doing anything at all?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... I suppose it's a bit of both, really. I just don't feel like doing much of anything. It's like the color has been drained out of everything, you know? Not just the mentoring. Even my morning coffee doesn't taste right anymore. I used to buy these specific beans from a little roaster down on 4th Street. The owner, Marcus, he travels to Colombia every year to source them directly. He was telling me once about the altitude and the soil acidity and how it completely changes the flavor profile. Fascinating stuff. I actually tried growing my own coffee plant in the sunroom a few years back, but the humidity in this part of the country just isn't right for it. The leaves all turned yellow and dropped off by November... \n\nBut anyway, yes. I just... I don't feel like doing anything at all. It's not that I don't like the mentoring anymore, it's just that the thought of getting dressed and going out and talking to people... it's just too much. I ended up going down to the cafeteria to get her a sandwich, but even that was wrapped in so much plastic it took me five minutes just to get it open. It reminds me of those TV dinners we used to have back in the seventies, you know? He always said the secret to a good day's work was a bowl of oatmeal and being up before the sun. I remember he had this old thermos, plaid, with a cork stopper, and he'd fill it with black tea... \n\nBut, uh, no. My sleep is fine. I haven't noticed any trouble with it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or felt like you’re not doing enough, especially in relation to your wife or other responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Guilty? 'Stopping by Woods on a Snowy Evening,' that was her favorite... \n\nAnyway, what was it? Guilt? No, I don't think I feel guilty. I just feel... tired, mostly. But not guilty, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve been moving more slowly than usual, or have others commented that you seem slowed down?\n\nPatient: Moving slower? No, I haven't really noticed anything like that. I get around alright. But you know who is moving slow these days? The construction crews on Interstate 95. I had to take a detour coming here today, and it was just a parking lot. They've had those orange barrels out since, gosh, April? It reminds me of this project I consulted on before I retired—I have my master's in urban planning, you know. We were doing a transit expansion over in the next county, and the zoning delays were just a nightmare. We had a proposal stuck in committee for three months because of a dispute over a historic retaining wall. The head of the historical society, this woman named Beatrice, used to come to every single town hall meeting with these giant binders full of sepia photographs. She had this one picture from 1912 of a horse-drawn carriage that had lost a wheel right in front of the wall... \n\nWait, sorry, I lost my train of thought. What did you ask me? Oh, right, me moving slow. No, no one's said I look slowed down or anything. I'm walking at the same pace I always do.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed any trouble concentrating or thinking clearly lately, like following conversations or making decisions?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Thinking clearly? No, I wouldn't say I've had any trouble there. My mind is as sharp as it ever was, and I make decisions just fine. Actually, I was just making a rather complicated decision yesterday about the garden. I was trying to decide between planting hydrangeas or azaleas along the back fence. You know, soil acidity is really the deciding factor with those. 

I had this neighbor back in the nineties, Arthur Pendelton, who tried to grow blue hydrangeas, but his soil was far too alkaline. They kept coming up this muddy, washed-out pink color, and he was just furious about it. He used to go out there with coffee grounds and pine needles by the bucketful, trying to change the pH. Arthur was quite a character, really. He drove this immaculate 1968 Chevrolet Impala, powder blue, not a single speck of rust on the entire body. He used to wash it every Sunday morning right in his driveway, listening to the baseball game on this little silver transistor radio he kept on the hood. It had one of those long metal antennas you had to pull out, and the announcer's voice would just echo down the street all morning... 

I'm sorry, I'm rambling again, aren't I? What was it you were asking? Oh, right, my concentration. No, I haven't had any trouble following conversations or anything like that. I'm perfectly clear.

I suppose it's a bit of both, really. I just don't feel like doing much of anything. It's like the color has been drained out of everything, you know? Not just the mentoring. Even my morning coffee doesn't taste right anymore. I used to buy these specific beans from a little roaster down on 4th Street. The owner, Marcus, he travels to Colombia every year to source them directly. He was telling me once about the altitude and the soil acidity and how it completely changes the flavor profile. Fascinating stuff. I actually tried growing my own coffee plant in the sunroom a few years back, but the humidity in this part of the country just isn't right for it. The leaves all turned yellow and dropped off by November... \n\nBut anyway, yes. I just... I don't feel like doing anything at all. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Thoughts about dying? Oh, goodness, no. Nothing like that. I want to be around for a good long while yet. There's so much left to see, isn't there? Actually, I was just reading an article in the Sunday paper about this fellow in Japan who just turned 112. Can you imagine? 112 years old. He attributed his longevity to eating fermented soybeans every single morning and walking three miles a day. 

My uncle Henry lived to be 98, you know. He never ate soybeans, though. He swore by a shot of whiskey and a raw egg before breakfast. I never could stomach the idea of a raw egg, myself. Henry was a fascinating man, though. He worked on the railroads back when they were switching from steam to diesel. He had this incredible collection of pocket watches—dozens of them, all different makes and models. He kept them in this velvet-lined display case he built out of mahogany. Every Sunday evening, he'd sit in his armchair by the fire, take out each watch one by one, and wind them. The ticking sound in that room was almost deafening, just a steady chorus of tiny gears and springs...

Oh, dear, I've wandered off again, haven't I? I apologize, my mind just catches on these little memories. What was the question? Dying? Right. No, no thoughts like that at all. I'm perfectly fine in that regard.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F32.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. How can I help you today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came in because... honestly, ever since my wife’s fall, I just don't know what's happening to me. I've always been the man with the lists and the plan. I mean, my whole career, I organized everything. Actually, I was just looking at my old leather planner the other day—they really don't make them like that anymore. The binding on the new ones always cracks after a year or two. I bought mine in Chicago back in '92 when I was presenting at a conference... wonderful city, Chicago, especially in the autumn. \n\nBut, um, anyway, what was I saying? Right. Since she fell, I've gone from being that organized guy to just... sitting at the kitchen table. Just staring at her medication chart for hours. I'll just start crying out of nowhere, or I'm snapping over absolutely nothing. And the mail is just sitting there. Bills are piling up. I usually do these mentoring calls for recent university graduates, and I've just let them all slide. Even the basic housework... it's all just piling up, and it's like I just can’t make myself function.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way since your wife’s fall?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s been about two months now. Yes, exactly two months. I remember because it was a Tuesday, and the garbage trucks were coming down our street right as the ambulance pulled up. Our garbage man, Frank... he's been doing our route for, gosh, must be fifteen years. He actually helped direct the paramedics to our front door. Really good guy, Frank. We were just talking the week before about his daughter starting at the state university, which is wonderful because they were so worried about the tuition costs, and I told him that the in-state options are just as rigorous if you look at the right departments... \n\nBut, um... wait. What was I... right, the timeline. Yes, about two months. Ever since that day she fell, everything at home has just completely changed, and I've been stuck like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve lost interest in things you used to enjoy, like mentoring or other activities?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, absolutely. I used to love those mentoring calls. I’d set aside every Thursday morning for them. But now... I just haven't been showing up. I haven't answered any of their emails either. And it's not just that. It's the house, too. I used to handle all the bills, the upkeep, the routines—just did it without even thinking. Now, the bills are just sitting in a pile on the entryway table. It’s a beautiful table, actually—solid mahogany, we found it at an estate sale in Oak Park back in... oh, it must have been '98 or '99. The craftsmanship on those older pieces is just remarkable. You don't see dovetail joints like that anymore, everything now is just held together with cheap glue and staples. I remember the man who sold it to us, he had the most fascinating collection of vintage pocket watches...\n\nBut, I'm sorry, I'm wandering again. Yes, the house. The routines are just slipping badly. I can't seem to manage the basic upkeep or the bills, and I've completely dropped all my community commitments and the mentoring. I just can't seem to care about any of it right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or in a low mood most of the time since your wife’s fall?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say I'm down all the time. It's more like... I'm just overwhelmed, you know? But not really depressed like they talk about on those commercials. Speaking of commercials, have you noticed how many of them are for medications these days? It's incredible. Every time I turn on the evening news—which I've been watching more of lately, usually the local broadcast with Jim Henderson. He's been the anchor for twenty years. I actually met him once at a charity dinner for the rotary club. He’s much taller in person than he looks on television, and he was telling this hilarious story about his teleprompter breaking right in the middle of a live broadcast... \n\nBut, um... what was the question? Oh, right, feeling down. No, I don't think it's that. It's more just the crying out of nowhere and snapping at things, like I mentioned. I just feel so out of control of everything.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you no longer get any pleasure or satisfaction out of things you used to enjoy, like mentoring or other activities?\n\nPatient: Well, like I said before... I just haven't been doing them at all. The mentoring, I mean. So I suppose I'm not getting pleasure out of it, because I'm not even there. I just... I don't feel the pull to do it. Which is a shame, really, because the young man I was mentoring, David, he was just starting to get the hang of the new software systems they use in the engineering department. When I was working, we did everything by hand, on drafting tables. You had to really know your geometry back then. I still have my old drafting set, actually, from when I was at university. The compass is solid brass, German-made. They just don't manufacture instruments with that kind of precision anymore. I was thinking of giving it to David, actually, as a graduation present, because he... \n\nWait, sorry. No, I'm just not doing the activities anymore. I just don't have the energy or the interest to even try right now. Everything just feels like too much effort.\n\nDoctor: When you say you don’t feel the pull to do the mentoring anymore, is it because you no longer enjoy it, or more that you just don’t feel like doing anything at all?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... I suppose it's a bit of both, really. I just don't feel like doing much of anything. It's like the color has been drained out of everything, you know? Not just the mentoring. Even my morning coffee doesn't taste right anymore. I used to buy these specific beans from a little roaster down on 4th Street. The owner, Marcus, he travels to Colombia every year to source them directly. He was telling me once about the altitude and the soil acidity and how it completely changes the flavor profile. Fascinating stuff. I actually tried growing my own coffee plant in the sunroom a few years back, but the humidity in this part of the country just isn't right for it. The leaves all turned yellow and dropped off by November... \n\nBut anyway, yes. I just... I don't feel like doing anything at all. It's not that I don't like the mentoring anymore, it's just that the thought of getting dressed and going out and talking to people... it's just too much. I'd rather just sit.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight since your wife’s fall?\n\nPatient: Well, my appetite... I haven't really noticed a big difference, to be honest. I mean, I'm still eating. But speaking of food, the hospital food they've been giving my wife is just atrocious. I don't know how anyone is expected to heal eating that sort of thing. Yesterday they brought her this... I think it was supposed to be meatloaf, but it looked more like a wet sponge. I ended up going down to the cafeteria to get her a sandwich, but even that was wrapped in so much plastic it took me five minutes just to get it open. It reminds me of those TV dinners we used to have back in the seventies, you know? The ones in the aluminum trays where the peas would always spill over into the cobbler... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, my weight. No, I don't think my weight has changed. I haven't stepped on a scale, but my trousers still fit the same.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep lately, like trouble falling asleep, waking up during the night, or sleeping more than usual?\n\nPatient: Sleep? No, I wouldn't say there's been any changes. I sleep about the same as I always have. I've never been one of those people who needs eight hours, anyway. My father was the same way. He used to wake up at four in the morning every single day, without an alarm clock, just to go down to the docks. He was a longshoreman for forty-five years. Worked right through the strike in '71. He always said the secret to a good day's work was a bowl of oatmeal and being up before the sun. I remember he had this old thermos, plaid, with a cork stopper, and he'd fill it with black tea... \n\nBut, uh, no. My sleep is fine. I haven't noticed any trouble with it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or felt like you’re not doing enough, especially in relation to your wife or other responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Guilty? No, I don't... I wouldn't say guilty. I mean, I'm doing what I can, going to the hospital and all. But hospitals, they really are something else these days. I was looking at the equipment in her room yesterday, and it's all touch screens and wireless monitors. It got me thinking about when my brother had his appendix out in, oh, it must have been 1982. The machines back then were massive, covered in dials and switches, making all sorts of racket. The nurses had to write everything down on these thick paper charts. I remember this one nurse, she had the most impeccable handwriting, like calligraphy. She told me she used to practice by copying out poems by Robert Frost. 'Stopping by Woods on a Snowy Evening,' that was her favorite... \n\nAnyway, what was it? Guilt? No, I don't think I feel guilty. I just feel... tired, mostly. But not guilty, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve been moving more slowly than usual, or have others commented that you seem slowed down?\n\nPatient: Moving slower? No, I haven't really noticed anything like that. I get around alright. But you know who is moving slow these days? The construction crews on Interstate 95. I had to take a detour coming here today, and it was just a parking lot. They've had those orange barrels out since, gosh, April? It reminds me of this project I consulted on before I retired—I have my master's in urban planning, you know. We were doing a transit expansion over in the next county, and the zoning delays were just a nightmare. We had a proposal stuck in committee for three months because of a dispute over a historic retaining wall. The head of the historical society, this woman named Beatrice, used to come to every single town hall meeting with these giant binders full of sepia photographs. She had this one picture from 1912 of a horse-drawn carriage that had lost a wheel right in front of the wall... \n\nWait, sorry, I lost my train of thought. What did you ask me? Oh, right, me moving slow. No, no one's said I look slowed down or anything. I'm walking at the same pace I always do.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble concentrating or thinking clearly lately, like following conversations or making decisions?\n\nPatient: Thinking clearly? No, I wouldn't say I've had any trouble there. My mind is as sharp as it ever was, and I make decisions just fine. Actually, I was just making a rather complicated decision yesterday about the garden. I was trying to decide between planting hydrangeas or azaleas along the back fence. You know, soil acidity is really the deciding factor with those. \n\nI had this neighbor back in the nineties, Arthur Pendelton, who tried to grow blue hydrangeas, but his soil was far too alkaline. They kept coming up this muddy, washed-out pink color, and he was just furious about it. He used to go out there with coffee grounds and pine needles by the bucketful, trying to change the pH. Arthur was quite a character, really. He drove this immaculate 1968 Chevrolet Impala, powder blue, not a single speck of rust on the entire body. He used to wash it every Sunday morning right in his driveway, listening to the baseball game on this little silver transistor radio he kept on the hood. It had one of those long metal antennas you had to pull out, and the announcer's voice would just echo down the street all morning... \n\nI'm sorry, I'm rambling again, aren't I? What was it you were asking? Oh, right, my concentration. No, I haven't had any trouble following conversations or anything like that. I'm perfectly clear.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling restless or slowed down, even if others haven't pointed it out?\n\nPatient: Restless? Slowed down? No, no, I don't think so. I mean, I feel like I'm moving just fine. I was actually just out walking this morning, down by the park with the duck pond. The ducks were certainly restless, though. There was this one mallard, a real aggressive sort, chasing all the others away from the breadcrumbs someone had left. \n\nIt reminded me of this goose we used to have at the lake house when I was a boy. We called him Barnaby. Barnaby was an absolute terror. He'd chase the mailman all the way down the driveway, honking and flapping his wings. My father had to put up a special fence just to keep him away from the front porch. Father built that fence himself, you know. He was a carpenter by trade, built half the houses on our street. Used this beautiful cedar wood that smelled just wonderful when it rained... \n\nI'm sorry, I'm doing it again, aren't I? I just get to talking and... what did you ask me? Restless, right. No, I haven't noticed feeling restless or slowed down at all. I'm doing alright in that department.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about death or dying, or wished you weren't alive?\n\nPatient: Thoughts about dying? Oh, goodness, no. Nothing like that. I want to be around for a good long while yet. There's so much left to see, isn't there? Actually, I was just reading an article in the Sunday paper about this fellow in Japan who just turned 112. He never ate soybeans, though. He swore by a shot of whiskey and a raw egg before breakfast. I never could stomach the idea of a raw egg, myself. Henry was a fascinating man, though. The ticking sound in that room was almost deafening, just a steady chorus of tiny gears and springs...\n\nOh, dear, I've wandered off again, haven't I? Harder to focus? Well, no, I wouldn't say that. I read the paper every morning, same as always. Just this morning I was reading the front page, actually. There was this fascinating piece about the city council debating the new zoning laws for the historic district. They're talking about restricting the paint colors people can use on those old Victorian homes down on Elm Street. 

It's funny, that reminds me of when my wife and I bought our first house back in '82. It wasn't a Victorian, just a little ranch-style place, but the previous owners had painted the kitchen this atrocious shade of mustard yellow. We spent our entire first weekend there trying to paint over it with a nice eggshell white, but that yellow just kept bleeding through. We must have put on four coats before it finally looked decent. My wife, she had this old pair of denim overalls she wore for painting, covered in splatters from every room we ever redecorated. She always said she was going to turn them into a quilt someday, but we never did get around to it. We kept them in the cedar chest at the foot of the bed...

Oh, I'm so sorry, I'm doing it again. I just get lost in these memories sometimes. You were asking about focusing on tasks, weren't you? Yes, well, no, I haven't noticed any real trouble with that. I can read the paper and talk to folks just fine.

I suppose it's a bit of both, really. I just don't feel like doing much of anything. It's like the color has been drained out of everything, you know? Not just the mentoring. Even my morning coffee doesn't taste right anymore. I used to buy these specific beans from a little roaster down on 4th Street. The owner, Marcus, he travels to Colombia every year to source them directly. He was telling me once about the altitude and the soil acidity and how it completely changes the flavor profile. Fascinating stuff. I actually tried growing my own coffee plant in the sunroom a few years back, but the humidity in this part of the country just isn't right for it. The leaves all turned yellow and dropped off by November... \n\nBut anyway, yes. I just... I don't feel like doing anything at all. It's not that I don't like the mentoring anymore, it's just that the thought of getting dressed and going out and talking to people... it's just too much. I haven't really noticed a big difference, to be honest. I mean, I'm still eating. But speaking of food, the hospital food they've been giving my wife is just atrocious. I don't know how anyone is expected to heal eating that sort of thing. Yesterday they brought her this... I think it was supposed to be meatloaf, but it looked more like a wet sponge. I ended up going down to the cafeteria to get her a sandwich, but even that was wrapped in so much plastic it took me five minutes just to get it open. It reminds me of those TV dinners we used to have back in the seventies, you know? The ones in the aluminum trays where the peas would always spill over into the cobbler... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, my weight. No, I don't think my weight has changed. I haven't stepped on a scale, but my trousers still fit the same.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep lately, like trouble falling asleep, waking up during the night, or sleeping more than usual?\n\nPatient: Sleep? No, I wouldn't say there's been any changes. I sleep about the same as I always have. I've never been one of those people who needs eight hours, anyway. My father was the same way. He used to wake up at four in the morning every single day, without an alarm clock, just to go down to the docks. He was a longshoreman for forty-five years. Worked right through the strike in '71. He always said the secret to a good day's work was a bowl of oatmeal and being up before the sun. I remember he had this old thermos, plaid, with a cork stopper, and he'd fill it with black tea... \n\nBut, uh, no. My sleep is fine. No, I don't... I wouldn't say guilty. I mean, I'm doing what I can, going to the hospital and all. But hospitals, they really are something else these days. I was looking at the equipment in her room yesterday, and it's all touch screens and wireless monitors. It got me thinking about when my brother had his appendix out in, oh, it must have been 1982. The machines back then were massive, covered in dials and switches, making all sorts of racket. The nurses had to write everything down on these thick paper charts. I remember this one nurse, she had the most impeccable handwriting, like calligraphy. She told me she used to practice by copying out poems by Robert Frost. 'Stopping by Woods on a Snowy Evening,' that was her favorite... \n\nAnyway, what was it? Guilt? No, I don't think I feel guilty. I just feel... tired, mostly. But not guilty, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve been moving more slowly than usual, or have others commented that you seem slowed down?\n\nPatient: Moving slower? No, I haven't really noticed anything like that. I get around alright. But you know who is moving slow these days? The construction crews on Interstate 95. I had to take a detour coming here today, and it was just a parking lot. They've had those orange barrels out since, gosh, April? It reminds me of this project I consulted on before I retired—I have my master's in urban planning, you know. We were doing a transit expansion over in the next county, and the zoning delays were just a nightmare. We had a proposal stuck in committee for three months because of a dispute over a historic retaining wall. The head of the historical society, this woman named Beatrice, used to come to every single town hall meeting with these giant binders full of sepia photographs. She had this one picture from 1912 of a horse-drawn carriage that had lost a wheel right in front of the wall... \n\nWait, sorry, I lost my train of thought. What did you ask me? I'm walking at the same pace I always do.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble concentrating or thinking clearly lately, like following conversations or making decisions?\n\nPatient: Thinking clearly? No, I wouldn't say I've had any trouble there. My mind is as sharp as it ever was, and I make decisions just fine. Actually, I was just making a rather complicated decision yesterday about the garden. I was trying to decide between planting hydrangeas or azaleas along the back fence. You know, soil acidity is really the deciding factor with those. \n\nI had this neighbor back in the nineties, Arthur Pendelton, who tried to grow blue hydrangeas, but his soil was far too alkaline. They kept coming up this muddy, washed-out pink color, and he was just furious about it. He used to go out there with coffee grounds and pine needles by the bucketful, trying to change the pH. Arthur was quite a character, really. He drove this immaculate 1968 Chevrolet Impala, powder blue, not a single speck of rust on the entire body. He used to wash it every Sunday morning right in his driveway, listening to the baseball game on this little silver transistor radio he kept on the hood. It had one of those long metal antennas you had to pull out, and the announcer's voice would just echo down the street all morning... \n\nI'm sorry, I'm rambling again, aren't I? What was it you were asking? Oh, right, my concentration. No, I haven't had any trouble following conversations or anything like that. I'm perfectly clear.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling restless or slowed down, even if others haven't pointed it out?\n\nPatient: Restless? Slowed down? No, no, I don't think so. I mean, I feel like I'm moving just fine. I was actually just out walking this morning, down by the park with the duck pond. The ducks were certainly restless, though. There was this one mallard, a real aggressive sort, chasing all the others away from the breadcrumbs someone had left. \n\nIt reminded me of this goose we used to have at the lake house when I was a boy. We called him Barnaby. Barnaby was an absolute terror. He'd chase the mailman all the way down the driveway, honking and flapping his wings. My father had to put up a special fence just to keep him away from the front porch. Father built that fence himself, you know. He was a carpenter by trade, built half the houses on our street. Used this beautiful cedar wood that smelled just wonderful when it rained... \n\nI'm sorry, I'm doing it again, aren't I? I just get to talking and... what did you ask me? Restless, right. No, I haven't noticed feeling restless or slowed down at all. I'm doing alright in that department.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about death or dying, or wished you weren't alive?\n\nPatient: Thoughts about dying? Oh, goodness, no. Nothing like that. I want to be around for a good long while yet. There's so much left to see, isn't there? Actually, I was just reading an article in the Sunday paper about this fellow in Japan who just turned 112. Can you imagine? 112 years old. He attributed his longevity to eating fermented soybeans every single morning and walking three miles a day. \n\nMy uncle Henry lived to be 98, you know. He never ate soybeans, though. He swore by a shot of whiskey and a raw egg before breakfast. I never could stomach the idea of a raw egg, myself. Henry was a fascinating man, though. He worked on the railroads back when they were switching from steam to diesel. He had this incredible collection of pocket watches—dozens of them, all different makes and models. He kept them in this velvet-lined display case he built out of mahogany. Every Sunday evening, he'd sit in his armchair by the fire, take out each watch one by one, and wind them. The ticking sound in that room was almost deafening, just a steady chorus of tiny gears and springs...\n\nOh, dear, I've wandered off again, haven't I? I apologize, my mind just catches on these little memories. What was the question? Dying? Right. No, no thoughts like that at all. I'm perfectly fine in that regard.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it harder to focus on tasks, like reading the newspaper or following a conversation, even for short periods?\n\nPatient: Harder to focus? Well, no, I wouldn't say that. I read the paper every morning, same as always. Just this morning I was reading the front page, actually. There was this fascinating piece about the city council debating the new zoning laws for the historic district. They're talking about restricting the paint colors people can use on those old Victorian homes down on Elm Street. \n\nIt's funny, that reminds me of when my wife and I bought our first house back in '82. It wasn't a Victorian, just a little ranch-style place, but the previous owners had painted the kitchen this atrocious shade of mustard yellow. We spent our entire first weekend there trying to paint over it with a nice eggshell white, but that yellow just kept bleeding through. We must have put on four coats before it finally looked decent. My wife, she had this old pair of denim overalls she wore for painting, covered in splatters from every room we ever redecorated. She always said she was going to turn them into a quilt someday, but we never did get around to it. We kept them in the cedar chest at the foot of the bed...\n\nOh, I'm so sorry, I'm doing it again. I just get lost in these memories sometimes. You were asking about focusing on tasks, weren't you? Yes, well, no, I haven't noticed any real trouble with that. I can read the paper and talk to folks just fine.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
